string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[]; onAttempt?: (choice: number | null) => void }) {\\r\\n  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<number | null>(null);\\r\\n  const id = useId();\\r\\n  const firstChoice = useRef<HTMLButtonElement>(null);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-question co-check\\"><h3 id={id}>{prompt}</h3><div className=\\"cr-answer-options\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-labelledby={id}>\\r\\n    {options.map((option, index) => <button ref={index === 0 ? firstChoice : undefined} type=\\"button\\" key={option} aria-pressed={choice === index} onClick={() => { setChoice(index); onAttempt?.(index); }}>{option}</button>)}\\r\\n  </div><div role=\\"status\\" aria-live=\\"polite\\">{choice !== null && <div className={`cr-feedback ${choice === correct ? \'cr-correct\' : \'cr-revise\'}`}><strong>{choice === correct ? \'Correct.\' : \'Not quite.\'}</strong><p>{feedback[choice]}</p></div>}</div>\\r\\n    {choice !== null && <button className=\\"cr-reset\\" type=\\"button\\" onClick={() => { firstChoice.current?.focus(); setChoice(null); onAttempt?.(null); }}>Try again</button>}\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction HypotheticalFigure() {\\r\\n  const ref = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);\\r\\n  const [inView, setInView] = useState(false);\\r\\n  useEffect(() => {\\r\\n    if (!ref.current || typeof IntersectionObserver === \'undefined\') return;\\r\\n    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => setInView(entry.isIntersecting), { threshold: .35 });\\r\\n    observer.observe(ref.current);\\r\\n    return () => observer.disconnect();\\r\\n  }, []);\\r\\n  return <figure className=\\"cr-hypothetical co-if-connection\\" ref={ref} data-in-view={inView}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-if\\"><span>IF</span><strong>this condition is true</strong><small>A case to imagine</small></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-connection\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><i/><i/><i/></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-then\\"><span>THEN</span><strong>the other condition has to be true</strong><small>What the rule establishes</small></div>\\r\\n    <figcaption>If the first condition is true, the other must be true. Imagining that case doesn\'t tell us it happened.</figcaption>\\r\\n  </figure>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\ntype FamilyEntry = { word: string; sentence: string; explanation: string };\\r\\nconst sufficientFamily: FamilyEntry[] = [\\r\\n  { word: \'if\', sentence: \'If someone borrows a locker, they have a key.\', explanation: \'Borrowing a locker is enough to establish that the person has a key.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'when / whenever\', sentence: \'Whenever someone borrows a locker, they have a key.\', explanation: \'Whenever covers every occasion of borrowing. A report that something happened once when someone borrowed a locker would not establish this general rule.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'any / anytime\', sentence: \'Any person who borrows a locker has a key.\', explanation: \'Pick any person meeting the borrowing condition. The rule gives that person a key. Anytime someone borrows a locker, they have a key expresses the same connection across occasions.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'all\', sentence: \'All people who borrow a locker have a key.\', explanation: \'All covers every person in the described group. Keep who borrow a locker with people; the rule is not about everyone.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'every / every time\', sentence: \'Every person who borrows a locker has a key.\', explanation: \'Every member of this group meets the other condition. Every time someone borrows a locker, they have a key preserves the relationship while speaking about occasions.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'in order to\', sentence: \'In order to borrow a locker, a person needs a key.\', explanation: \'Borrowing the locker is the goal. Having a key is its requirement. This does not say that having a key is enough to borrow a locker.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'people who\', sentence: \'People who borrow a locker have a key.\', explanation: \'People who borrow a locker names the complete group being described. For a person in that group, having a key follows.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'each\', sentence: \'Each person who borrows a locker has a key.\', explanation: \'Each applies the statement to every individual locker borrower. We have changed the wording, not what borrowing establishes.\' },\\r\\n];\\r\\nconst necessaryFamily: FamilyEntry[] = [\\r\\n  { word: \'then\', sentence: \'If someone borrows a locker, then they have a key.\', explanation: \'Then introduces what the borrowing condition establishes: the person has a key.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'must\', sentence: \'A person borrowing a locker must have a key.\', explanation: \'Here we are describing a requirement under the stated rule. If must appears in an instruction instead, preserve that meaning: a required action is not proof that someone completed it.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'necessary\', sentence: \'Having a key is necessary for borrowing a locker.\', explanation: \'Ask necessary for what? For borrowing a locker. Having a key is the requirement, not a promise that someone borrowed.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'required\', sentence: \'Having a key is required for borrowing a locker.\', explanation: \'Required names the same relationship. Every locker borrower meets the key requirement.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'only / only if\', sentence: \'A person borrows a locker only if they have a key.\', explanation: \'Only if makes having a key necessary. Only people with a key borrow a locker says the same thing. Neither gives a locker to every person with a key.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'depends\', sentence: \'Borrowing a locker depends on having a key.\', explanation: \'Having a key is what the borrowing depends on. The sentence does not make the key enough by itself.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'need / need to\', sentence: \'A person needs a key to borrow a locker.\', explanation: \'What does the person need? A key. A person needs to have a key to borrow a locker expresses the same requirement.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'have to\', sentence: \'A person has to have a key to borrow a locker.\', explanation: \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Read have to have a key as one complete requirement.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'essential\', sentence: \'Having a key is essential for borrowing a locker.\', explanation: \'Essential for borrowing names the requirement. The borrowing cannot meet this rule without the key.\' },\\r\\n  { word: \'precondition\', sentence: \'Having a key is a precondition for borrowing a locker.\', explanation: \'A precondition is something the other condition requires. Do not infer that the requirement was sufficient, or invent a timeline from the label.\' },\\r\\n];\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function WordFamily({ entries, role }: { entries: FamilyEntry[]; role: \'sufficient\' | \'necessary\' }) {\\r\\n  const [selected, setSelected] = useState<number | null>(null);\\r\\n  const regionId = useId();\\r\\n  const firstExpression = useRef<HTMLButtonElement>(null);\\r\\n  return <div className={`cr-family co-lab cr-family-${role}`}>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"cr-instruction\\">Choose an expression. Read its sentence before comparing the explanation.</p>\\r\\n    <ol className=\\"cr-family-list\\">{entries.map((entry, index) => <li key={entry.word}><button ref={index === 0 ? firstExpression : undefined} type=\\"button\\" aria-pressed={selected === index} aria-controls={regionId} onClick={() => setSelected(index)}><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">{String(index + 1).padStart(2, \'0\')}</span>{entry.word}</button></li>)}</ol>\\r\\n    <div id={regionId} className=\\"cr-family-reading\\" aria-live=\\"polite\\">{selected === null ? <p>The words vary. We\'ll keep the same locker relationship while reading each one.</p> : <><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">{entries[selected].word}</p><blockquote>{entries[selected].sentence}</blockquote><p>{entries[selected].explanation}</p><div className=\\"cr-family-role\\"><span>{role === \'sufficient\' ? \'Enough to establish the other condition\' : \'Required for the other condition\'}</span><strong>{role === \'sufficient\' ? \'Borrows a locker\' : \'Has a key\'}</strong></div></>}</div>\\r\\n    {selected !== null && <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"cr-reset\\" onClick={() => { firstExpression.current?.focus(); setSelected(null); }}>Clear the example</button>}\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst cases = [\\r\\n  { label: \'Sufficient present\', fact: \'You win the raffle.\', question: \'What do we know about getting a bicycle?\', options: [\'You get a bicycle.\', \\"You don\'t get a bicycle.\\", \'Getting a bicycle is not settled.\'], correct: 0, reasoning: [\'Winning meets the sufficient condition. Under the rule, you get a bicycle.\', \'Winning without getting a bicycle would violate the rule. Winning establishes the promised result.\', \'The rule covers you if you win. Getting a bicycle is settled here.\'] },\\r\\n  { label: \'Necessary absent\', fact: \\"You don\'t get a bicycle.\\", question: \'What do we know about winning the raffle?\', options: [\'You win the raffle.\', \\"You don\'t win the raffle.\\", \'Winning the raffle is not settled.\'], correct: 1, reasoning: [\'If you won, you would get a bicycle. That conflicts with the given outcome.\', \'The missing requirement rules out winning. Winning without getting a bicycle cannot fit the rule.\', \\"We can settle it. Winning would require getting a bicycle, and that result doesn\'t occur.\\"] },\\r\\n  { label: \'Sufficient absent\', fact: \\"You don\'t win the raffle.\\", question: \'What do we know about getting a bicycle?\', options: [\'You get a bicycle.\', \\"You don\'t get a bicycle.\\", \'Getting a bicycle is not settled.\'], correct: 2, reasoning: [\\"You could get one by buying it or receiving a gift. But the rule doesn\'t establish that you do.\\", \\"You could buy a bicycle without winning. The rule hasn\'t made the raffle the only way to get one.\\", \\"Both getting a bicycle without winning and not getting one without winning fit the rule. Getting a bicycle remains open.\\"] },\\r\\n  { label: \'Necessary present\', fact: \'You get a bicycle.\', question: \'What do we know about winning the raffle?\', options: [\'You win the raffle.\', \\"You don\'t win the raffle.\\", \'Winning the raffle is not settled.\'], correct: 2, reasoning: [\\"You might get it by winning, but you could buy it or receive it as a gift. Getting a bicycle alone doesn\'t establish winning.\\", \\"You could win and get a bicycle. Nothing in the fact rules out winning.\\", \'You could get a bicycle with or without winning the raffle. Having the required result does not establish the sufficient condition.\'] },\\r\\n];\\r\\nfunction FourCaseExplorer() {\\r\\n  const [selected, setSelected] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [answers, setAnswers] = useState<Record<number, number>>({});\\r\\n  const current = cases[selected];\\r\\n  const answer = answers[selected];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-cases co-lab\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">One rule. Four starting facts.</p><blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-case-tabs\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Choose a starting fact\\">{cases.map((item, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={item.label} aria-pressed={selected === index} onClick={() => setSelected(index)}><span>{String(index + 1).padStart(2, \'0\')}</span>{item.label}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-case-fact\\"><span>Given</span><strong>{current.fact}</strong></div><h3>{current.question}</h3>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-answer-options\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Choose what follows\\">{current.options.map((option, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={option} aria-pressed={answer === index} onClick={() => setAnswers({ ...answers, [selected]: index })}>{option}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <div role=\\"status\\">{answer !== undefined && <div className={`cr-feedback ${answer === current.correct ? \'cr-correct\' : \'cr-revise\'}`}><strong>{answer === current.correct ? \'Correct.\' : \'Not quite.\'}</strong><p>{current.reasoning[answer]}</p></div>}</div>\\r\\n    {Object.keys(answers).length === 4 && <div className=\\"cr-case-review\\"><h3>Keep these four distinctions</h3><dl>{cases.map(item => <div key={item.label}><dt>{item.label}</dt><dd>{item.options[item.correct]}</dd></div>)}</dl><p>The rule still holds in all four cases. Two facts give an inference; two leave the other condition open.</p></div>}\\r\\n    <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"cr-reset\\" onClick={() => { setAnswers({}); setSelected(0); }}>Reset the four cases</button>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction ContrapositiveDiscovery() {\\r\\n  const [attempted, setAttempted] = useState(false);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cr-discovery\\"><p>Use this stated rule for a different case.</p><blockquote>If a report is in the published archive, it has an approval stamp.</blockquote>\\r\\n    <p>A report in that archive has to carry the stamp. Now you inspect Report K. It has no approval stamp.</p>\\r\\n    <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Could Report K be in the published archive while this rule holds?\\" options={[\'Yes, its location is unrestricted.\', \'No, it cannot be in that archive.\', \'Yes, the missing stamp puts it in the archive.\']} correct={1} onAttempt={choice => setAttempted(choice !== null)} feedback={[\'Its location is restricted by the rule. If K were in the archive, it would have a stamp, which conflicts with the given fact.\', \'An archived K would have to have a stamp. K has no stamp, so it cannot be in the published archive under this rule.\', \'The missing stamp rules out archive membership. It does not establish it.\']}/>\\r\\n    {attempted && <div className=\\"cr-discovery-reasoning\\"><p>That way of using the rule is the <strong>contrapositive</strong>: when the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition cannot be present.</p>\\r\\n      <p>It is the same relationship. We didn\'t add a second rule; we worked out a consequence already contained in the first one.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>{raffleRule}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>Here, “you don\'t get a bicycle” means you never receive the promised outcome. It does not mean you\'re still waiting for a prize that will arrive later.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"You don\'t get a bicycle. What follows about winning the raffle?\\" options={[\\"You don\'t win the raffle.\\", \'You win the raffle.\', \'Nothing follows about winning.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Winning would require getting a bicycle. Since that outcome does not occur, you cannot have won under the rule.\', \'Winning without getting a bicycle would break the rule. The absent requirement excludes winning.\', \'The missing requirement does give an inference. If you won, you would get a bicycle.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Don\'t confuse that with starting from a …2787 tokens truncated…and <strong>only if</strong> have different jobs; read only if as the whole expression.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A person borrows a locker only if they have a key. Which condition goes on the right?\\" options={[\'Borrows a locker\', \'Has a key\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Borrowing is what has the requirement. Ask what a locker borrower must have: a key. Borrowing goes left; having a key goes right.\', \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Only if appears in the middle, but it still identifies the necessary condition.\']}/>\\r\\n      <h3>The indicator can also follow its target</h3>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The target is the complete idea the indicator refers to. It comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. We find it by asking what is required, not by taking whichever words come next.</p>\\r\\n      <p>In your Group 1 and Group 2 lessons, compare what each expression tells you about the same case. If you know someone borrows a locker, a key follows. Knowing that person has a key leaves borrowing open. Different wording should preserve that distinction.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Let\'s build an original diagram slowly. First find the indicator, then draw the arrow, place the complete idea it refers to, and fill the other side.</p>\\r\\n      <IncrementalWriter/>\\r\\n      <p>Now move the if-clause: “You get a bicycle if you win the raffle.” The winning condition comes last in English but still goes left in the diagram. Its job has not changed.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Which diagram preserves the moved if-clause?\\" options={[\'Get bicycle → Win\', \'Win → Get bicycle\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You let sentence order choose the arrow. The if-clause still makes winning enough for getting a bicycle.\', \'The sufficient condition is still winning. Where it appears in the sentence does not change what it establishes.\']}/>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={9} label=\\"Write and read the contrapositive\\" title=\\"Write the meaning you already understand\\">\\r\\n      <p>Win means you win the raffle. Get bicycle means you get a bicycle. Our original is Win → Get bicycle.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We already know that not getting a bicycle rules out winning. Write that as <strong>~Get bicycle → ~Win</strong>. The <strong>~</strong> sign means <strong>not</strong>. Not getting the outcome is different from not having received it yet.</p>\\r\\n      <p>To write a contrapositive from a diagram, reverse the two sides and negate <strong>both complete conditions</strong>. A condition already containing not loses that not when we negate it. Let\'s try all four patterns.</p>\\r\\n      <SignPatterns/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-method\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">The complete five-step routine</p><ol><li><strong>Find the indicator.</strong> Read its job in the whole sentence.</li><li><strong>Identify both complete ideas.</strong> Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.</li><li><strong>Place the roles.</strong> Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.</li><li><strong>Write the contrapositive.</strong> Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.</li><li><strong>Read both in English.</strong> Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.</li></ol></div>\\r\\n      <p>The original and its contrapositive describe the same relationship. If writing both helps you see that relationship, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn\'t copy it merely to have a second line.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition\'s absence and work back to the left condition\'s absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={10} label=\\"Practical diagramming questions\\" title=\\"Use writing to make your reasoning easier to check\\">\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-faq\\">\\r\\n        <details><summary>When should I diagram?</summary><p>While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the relationship on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How do I check whether my diagram is right?</summary><p>Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>What if writing takes time?</summary><p>Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the sentences you are actually working through. First make the relationship accurate. Work on speed once you can explain why the arrow follows. </p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>Should I always write the contrapositive?</summary><p>You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.</p></details>\\r\\n        <aside className=\\"cr-training-note\\"><h3>Why this practice asks for both</h3><p>In the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We\'re checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.</p></aside>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How should I abbreviate the conditions?</summary><p>Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. W might mean win this raffle. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you\'re tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.</p></details>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?\\" options={[\'The labels need to be longer.\', \'The arrow makes the requirement enough.\', \'Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Longer labels would not repair the direction. The tray requires a paid stamp, but a stamped envelope can remain outside it.\', \'Only restricts tray envelopes to ones with paid stamps. In tray → Paid stamp preserves that requirement; the proposed reverse promises tray placement to every stamped envelope.\', \'Sentence order does not determine diagram order. Only identifies the paid stamp as required for tray placement.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>That error came from the language. Let\'s inspect precisely which idea an indicator refers to.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={11} label=\\"Find the target\\" title=\\"Which reports can be published?\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>In the first sentence below, only names reports with editor approval. In the next, editor approval comes before necessary. Choose the words that identify what publication requires in each sentence.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>“Editor approval is required for publication.”</p><p>“Publication requires editor approval.”</p><span>Same requirement, different placement</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Publication needs editor approval in all three sentences. Approval can appear before or after the indicator without changing that requirement. Publication therefore goes on the left and editor approval on the right.</p>\\r\\n      <p>When the wording still feels uncertain, we can test a proposed translation against an imagined case.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={12} label=\\"Check an interpretation\\" title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong> lets you test what a proposed sufficient condition would establish. First read the ordinary meaning. Propose the sufficient idea. Imagine a case meeting it. Ask what the sentence now requires.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Only people wearing helmets ride the studio bikes.</blockquote><p>The sentence restricts riders to people wearing helmets. Keep that meaning while trying each proposed starting point.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-personalized\\"><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">First proposed starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m riding a studio bike, what does this sentence tell me?\\" options={[\'I am wearing a helmet.\', \'The helmet question is open.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence puts every rider in the helmet-wearing group. Riding → Wearing helmet preserves that meaning.\', \'Only restricts who rides. If you meet the riding condition, the sentence does settle helmet wearing.\']}/></div><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Try the other starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m wearing a helmet, must I be riding a studio bike?\\" options={[\'Yes, the helmet proves riding.\', \'No, I could wear one without riding.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You gave every helmet wearer a ride. The sentence restricts riders; it does not make all helmet wearers riders.\', \'A helmet wearer waiting beside a bike fits the sentence. Wearing helmet → Riding would add a promise the original did not make.\']}/></div></div>\\r\\n      <p>The first reading matches the sentence. The second proposed arrow fails because it rules out a case the original permits. Use the imagined situation to inspect meaning, not to supply extra facts from what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Use the What Test beside it</h3><p>Only what? People wearing helmets. People alone would drop the helmet restriction. The complete phrase tells us which people can ride a studio bike. Riding establishes that the rider wears a helmet; wearing one still doesn\'t establish riding.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The What Test keeps wearing a helmet attached to people. The Personalized If/Then lets you check the arrow: imagine you ride a studio bike, then ask what the sentence requires you to know. If your diagram instead promises a ride to everyone wearing a helmet, it has changed the rule.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'ve now read the relationship, understood its limits, written both directions and checked the language. Put those pieces together with fresh sentences.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={13} label=\\"Use the whole process\\" title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n      <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You can look back. The job here is to produce and inspect the reasoning, not to prove you memorized every expression on the first reading. The labels below keep the complete ideas visible while you choose the directions.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-integrated-grid\\">{referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} key={item.sentence}/>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n    <section id=\\"reference-boundary\\" className=\\"cr-boundary co-section\\" data-reference-boundary data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Permission and instructions\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Before the attached practice</p><h2>Keep what the sentence actually gives you</h2>\\r\\n      <p>The core process is now in place. One more distinction matters for the practice: a conditional can give permission or an instruction. Preserve that strength instead of turning it into a report of what someone did.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote><p>May use gives permission here. A ticketed guest is allowed to use the cloakroom. The guest could keep their coat on and never go inside.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Lena is a guest with a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\" options={[\'Yes, the ticket establishes that she used it.\', \'No, we know she is allowed to use it.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned permission into a completed action. Lena meets the condition for permission, but we have no fact reporting her use of the cloakroom.\', \'The rule gives Lena permission because she is a guest with a ticket. Whether she used the cloakroom remains open.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Now compare an instruction. “If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack” tells you what to do with a wet coat. It does not report that someone followed the instruction.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice has not reported that anyone returned this tray.\', \'The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone returned it is a separate question.\', \'The instruction asks for a return. It does not say a return is impossible.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Allowed to use isn\'t used. Told to return isn\'t returned. For someone in that group, having a key follows. Borrowing goes left.</p></div><div><h3>Right: necessary</h3><p>This is what the sufficient condition requires. Group 2 expressions such as <strong>only if, requires</strong> and <strong>must</strong> can identify the requirement.</p><p>“Borrowing a locker requires a key.” What is required? <strong>Having a key.</strong> That condition goes right.</p></div></div>\\r\\nsrc/bootcamps/conditionals/components/ConditionalReferenceSequence.tsx:374:      <p>In your Group 1 and Group 2 lessons, compare what each expression tells you about the same case. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\nsrc/bootcamps/conditionals/components/ConditionalReferenceSequence.tsx:470:          <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"4a5ba4","wall_time_seconds":0.3057258,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3340,"output":"---\\r\\ntitle: Teaching Voice\\r\\ntype: concept\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\\r\\ncreated: 2026-06-10\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nstatus: active\\r\\nsubject: Teaching Voice\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Teaching Voice\\r\\n\\r\\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core voice (every register)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\r\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\r\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\r\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\r\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\\r\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\"; \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\r\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\r\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\r\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\r\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\r\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The apology / accountability move\\r\\n\\r\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Hi Sandra,\\r\\n>\\r\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\r\\n> [link]\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Best,\\r\\n> Joshua\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\r\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\r\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\r\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\r\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\r\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\r\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\r\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\r\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Situational micro-templates\\r\\n\\r\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\r\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\r\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\r\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\r\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\r\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\r\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\r\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The three registers\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\r\\n\\r\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\r\\n\\r\\nReference line (target tone):\\r\\n\\r\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\\r\\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where it slipped.\\r\\n- **Accountability (student isn\'t doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never \\"the reps aren\'t happening,\\" never tough love.\\r\\n- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.\\r\\n  > That\'s the cleanest you\'ve split premise from conclusion yet. Let\'s push it into the harder sets.\\r\\n- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. \\"Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Teaching content (conversational reasoning) -- lessons, recaps, notes, drills, explanations\\r\\n\\r\\nUse [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md), Joshua\'s selected calibration from 2026-10-02. Keep the reader beside the teacher. Explain the ordinary meaning, ground it in an original example, make the small inferences audible, ask a useful question, and show both the consequence and its limit. Introduce terminology and notation when their meaning is established. Give each concept enough development to be learned from the page.\\r\\n\\r\\nContractions and varied sentence lengths should sound natural when read aloud. Repetition should change the reader\'s view of the same relationship. Questions should give the reader actual thinking work. Light humor may appear within an explanation when it helps understanding; this supersedes the older wit-only-at-the-edges restriction. Do not force punchlines, slang, hyperbole, catchphrases, or paragraph quotas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source governs delivery, not Joshua\'s procedure, indicator taxonomy, biography, or the scope of a lesson. Preserve his approved methods and logical precision. Personal encouragement still follows the earnest coaching register. Project-owned `lsat-u-voice` routes here and to the detailed standard; an older vendor writer-blend skill must not override this selection.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\\r\\n\\r\\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed \\"the next card is the page in front of you\\" was clever but empty).\\r\\n\\r\\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\\r\\n\\r\\nBrand copy speaks as the platform (\\"we\\", \\"you\\"), not as an individual (\\"I\\", \\"Joshua\\"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Don\'t\\r\\n\\r\\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\\r\\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\r\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\r\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\r\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\r\\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"93ffdf","wall_time_seconds":0.5205627,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2806,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 16.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"ed70d4","wall_time_seconds":0.4197164,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":69,"output":"src/bootcamps/conditionals/components\\\\OpeningCourseContent.tsx:112:  return <section className=\\"co-section cr-course-section ma-lesson\\" data-reference-source={8}>\\r\\nsrc/bootcamps/conditionals/components\\\\OpeningCourseContent.tsx:118:        <div className=\\"ma-relationship\\">\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"8978eb","wall_time_seconds":0.5393868,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2097,"output":"      <p>We have seen what can be enough. Now let\'s name what that condition requires.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={4} label=\\"The necessary condition\\" title=\\"Necessary means required\\">\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-sufficiency nc-lesson\\">\\r\\n        <p className=\\"cr-sufficiency-lead\\"><strong>A necessary condition is a requirement. It has to be met, but meeting it might not be enough.</strong></p>\\r\\n        <p>For example, you have to be a student to be enrolled in law school. That makes being a student <strong>necessary for law-school enrollment</strong>.</p>\\r\\n        <NecessaryConditionStatement/>\\r\\n        <p>But being a student doesn’t mean you’re enrolled in law school. You could be studying history instead.</p>\\r\\n        <p className=\\"nc-takeaway\\"><strong>Being a student is required. It doesn’t establish enrollment on its own.</strong></p>\\r\\n        <NecessaryConditionExperience/>\\r\\n        <section className=\\"cr-sufficient-explanation\\" aria-label=\\"Why a requirement is not necessarily enough\\">\\r\\n          <h3>Two roles in the same relationship</h3>\\r\\n          <p>Compare the two roles: <strong>being enrolled is enough to establish being a student; being a student is required for being enrolled.</strong> These describe the same relationship from different sides.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Whenever the sufficient condition is true, the necessary condition must be true too. The rule does not tell us that either condition is actually true of a particular person.</p>\\r\\n          <details className=\\"cr-extension\\"><summary>Can a condition be both necessary and sufficient?</summary><p>Yes, when the relationship supports both directions. Necessary means required; it doesn\'t mean never enough. Our rule tells us that enrollment requires being a student. It doesn\'t say that being a student is enough to establish enrollment.</p></details>\\r\\n        </section>\\r\\n        <SufficientConditionFilm key=\\"necessary-definition\\" assets={necessaryConditionFilms} title=\\"Why the requirement is not enough here\\" ariaLabel=\\"Short explanation of necessity\\"/>\\r\\n        <section className=\\"cr-sufficient-explanation sc-indicator-list\\" aria-label=\\"Group 2 indicator words\\">\\r\\n          <h3>Group 2: Necessary-condition indicators</h3>\\r\\n          <p>These words and phrases can help you find a requirement. Read the whole sentence, then identify <strong>what is required and what requires it</strong>.</p>\\r\\n          <ul>{[\'only\', \'only if\', \'only when\', \'only where\', \'always\', \'requires\', \'must\'].map(word => <li key={word}>{word}</li>)}</ul>\\r\\n          <h3>Different wording, the same requirement</h3>\\r\\n          <p>The first six sentences describe our law-school relationship. In each one, ask what a person enrolled in law school has to be. Then we\'ll use a place example for only where.</p>\\r\\n          <dl className=\\"nc-indicator-examples\\">\\r\\n            <div><dt>Requires</dt><dd><blockquote>Being enrolled in law school <strong>requires</strong> being a student.</blockquote><p>What does enrollment require? <strong>Being a student.</strong> Requires is the indicator; being a student is the condition it identifies. They are not the same thing.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n            <div><dt>Must</dt><dd><blockquote>If you are enrolled in law school, you <strong>must</strong> be a student.</blockquote><p>Must makes the certainty explicit: enrollment cannot coexist with not being a student. Here it describes what has to be true, not a task someone has been told to complete.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n            <div><dt>Only</dt><dd><blockquote><strong>Only</strong> students are enrolled in law school.</blockquote><p>This excludes nonstudents from the law-school group. It does not put every student in that group. Being a student remains required; being enrolled remains enough to establish it.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n            <div><dt>Only if</dt><dd><blockquote>You are enrolled in law school <strong>only if</strong> you are a student.</blockquote><p>Only if introduces the requirement: <strong>you are a student</strong>. Read the whole phrase. The if inside only if does not make being a student sufficient.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n            <div><dt>Only when</dt><dd><blockquote>You are enrolled in law school <strong>only when</strong> you are a student.</blockquote><p>On any occasion when enrollment is true, being a student must be true too. This does not say you become enrolled whenever you are a student, or that one condition has to happen later than the other.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n            <div><dt>Always</dt><dd><blockquote>While you are enrolled in law school, you are <strong>always</strong> a student.</blockquote><p>Always allows no exception within the situation described: being enrolled in law school. It does not say every student is enrolled, or that someone remains a student after enrollment ends.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n            <div><dt>Only where</dt><dd><p>This expression restricts places, so use a setting example:</p><blockquote>A seminar takes place <strong>only where</strong> a tutor is present.</blockquote><p>At any place with that seminar, <strong>a tutor must be present</strong>. A tutor could also be somewhere without a seminar. A seminar taking place is sufficient to establish a tutor\'s presence there; the tutor\'s presence is necessary for the seminar.</p></dd></div>\\r\\n          </dl>\\r\\n          <h3>Why only if is different from if</h3>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"nc-role-comparison\\">\\r\\n            <div><h4>If you are enrolled in law school, you are a student.</h4><p><strong>If</strong> identifies enrollment as enough. This is our original relationship.</p></div>\\r\\n            <div><h4>You are enrolled in law school only if you are a student.</h4><p><strong>Only if</strong> identifies being a student as required. This preserves that same relationship.</p></div>\\r\\n          </div>\\r\\n          <p>Now remove only from the second sentence: “You are enrolled in law school <strong>if</strong> you are a student.” That would make being a student enough for law-school enrollment. A history student who is not enrolled in law school shows why that is a different claim.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Ask <strong>what the sentence requires</strong>. Identify the complete condition, including any restrictions. The next example gives you a new relationship to check.</p>\\r\\n        </section>\\r\\n        <section className=\\"cr-sufficient-explanation nc-checks\\" aria-label=\\"Apply the necessary condition to a new rule\\">\\r\\n          <h3>Find the requirement in a new rule</h3>\\r\\n          <blockquote>An application is accepted for review only if it includes a current reference letter.</blockquote>\\r\\n          <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"What is required for an application to be accepted for review?\\" options={[\'Including a current reference letter.\', \'Including any reference letter, even an outdated one.\', \'Being accepted for review.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'Only if identifies including a current reference letter as required. An application accepted for review cannot be missing that current letter.\', \'Current matters. An outdated letter does not meet the requirement in this rule. Keep that word when you identify what is required.\', \'Acceptance is the condition that requires the letter. Including a current reference letter is the requirement. You have swapped the two roles.\']}/>\\r\\n          <h3>Does meeting the requirement guarantee acceptance?</h3>\\r\\n          <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Does including a current reference letter guarantee that the application is accepted for review?\\" options={[\'Yes. The letter is all the application needs.\', \'No. The letter is required, but the rule does not say it is enough.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned a requirement into a guarantee. The rule tells us that accepted applications include a current letter, not that every application with one is accepted.\', \'An application with a current letter could be accepted or not accepted. Both fit the rule. Having the required letter alone does not establish acceptance.\']}/>\\r\\n        </section>\\r\\n        <aside className=\\"cr-sufficient-bridge\\"><h3>Next: Put the relationship into a diagram</h3><p>We now have both roles: the condition that guarantees, and the condition it requires. Next, we\'ll use an arrow to record that connection.</p></aside>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <p>Next, record the relationship with the must arrow.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}, Their stateful exercises are\\r\\n// mounted by React; the former reader-page renderer is never invoked here.\\r\\nfunction sections(children: ReactNode): ContentSection[] {\\r\\n  return Children.toArray(children).flatMap(child => {\\r\\n    if (!isValidElement<ContentSection[\'props\']>(child)) return [];\\r\\n    return child.type === Fragment ? sections(child.props.children) : [child];\\r\\n  });\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction reference(number: number): ContentSection {\\r\\n  const section = sections(conditionalReferenceContent().props.children).find(item => item.props.number === number);\\r\\n  if (!section) throw new Error(`Missing approved conditional reference section ${number}`);\\r\\n  return section;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction groupSections(group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\') {\\r\\n  return sections(GroupLessons({ id: group }).props.children);\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction groupSection(group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\', title: string) {\\r\\n  const section = groupSections(group).find(item => item.props.id === title || item.props.title === title);\\r\\n  if (!section) throw new Error(`Missing approved conditional section: ${title}`);\\r\\n  const sectionId = `course-${group}-${title.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]+/g, \'-\')}`;\\r\\n  return cloneElement(section, { key: sectionId, id: sectionId });\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction NoteSection({ title, children }: { title: string; children: ReactNode }) {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section\\"><div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}</section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction ReferenceBody({ number, omitTransition = false }: { number: number; omitTransition?: boolean }) {\\r\\n  const source = reference(number);\\r\\n  const children = Children.toArray(source.props.children);\\r\\n  // These source sections end in navigation prose for the former ordering.\\r\\n  // The course shell now owns transitions; the teaching stays in its source.\\r\\n  return <section className={`co-section cr-course-section ${source.props.dark ? \'cr-dark\' : \'\'}`} data-reference-source={number}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>{source.props.title}</h2></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-step-body\\">{omitTransition ? children.slice(0, -1) : children}</div>\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction FamilySupplement({ group }: { group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\' }) {\\r\\n  const source = reference(group === \'group-1\' ? 3 : 5);\\r\\n  const family = sections(source.props.children).find(child => child.type === WordFamily);\\r\\n  if (!family) throw new Error(`Missing approved ${group} supplementary family`);\\r\\n  return <details className=\\"co-practical-note\\"><summary>More expressions for the same relationship</summary>\\r\\n    <p>Use the stated rule: anyone who borrows a locker has a key. These sentences preserve that connection. They do not say every person with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n    {family}\\r\\n  </details>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// Only and only if both mark the requirement, from different places in the sentence.\\r\\nfunction OnlyAndOnlyIf() {\\r\\n  return <NoteSection title=\\"Only and only if can mark the same requirement\\">\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>Only</strong> [people with a key] borrow a locker.</p><p>A person borrows a locker <strong>only if</strong> [they have a key].</p><span>Only and only if identify the necessary condition here.</span></div>\\r\\n    <p>Both sentences say locker borrowers have a key. In the first, only is at the beginning. In the second, only if is in the middle. Having a key is required in both, so it goes right. Read only if as the whole expression; the if inside it isn\'t doing the job if does on its own.</p>\\r\\n    <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A person borrows a locker only if they have a key. Which condition goes on the right?\\" options={[\'Borrows a locker\', \'Has a key\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Borrowing is what has the requirement. Ask what a locker borrower must have: a key. Borrowing goes left; having a key goes right.\', \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Only if appears in the middle, but it still identifies the necessary condition.\']}/>\\r\\n  </NoteSection>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction IndicatorNote({ group }: { group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\' }) {\\r\\n  const [selected, setSelected] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [opened, setOpened] = useState<number[]>([0]);\\r\\n  const [focusPanel, setFocusPanel] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const panels = useRef<(HTMLDivElement | null)[]>([]);\\r\\n  const id = useId();\\r\\n  const content = groupSections(group);\\r\\n  const expressions = content.slice(2, 9);\\r\\n  const names = expressions.map(section => section.props.title?.split(\':\')[0]);\\r\\n  useEffect(() => {\\r\\n    if (!focusPanel) return;\\r\\n    panels.current[selected]?.focus();\\r\\n    setFocusPanel(false);\\r\\n  }, [focusPanel, selected]);\\r\\n  const choose = (index: number, moveFocus = false) => {\\r\\n    setSelected(index);\\r\\n    setOpened(previous => previous.includes(index) ? previous : [...previous, index]);\\r\\n    setFocusPanel(moveFocus);\\r\\n  };\\r\\n  const last = selected === expressions.length - 1;\\r\\n  // Two introductions and the seven complete expression explanations belong\\r\\n  // together in one concept note, not nine separate navigation destinations.\\r\\n  return <>{content.slice(0, 2)}\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-word-guide\\">Work through all seven expressions. Each one does the same job in a different sentence, and each has its own trap to check. Use Next expression to go in order, or choose any expression to read it again.</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-word-tabs\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Choose an expression\\">{expressions.map((section, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={section.props.title} aria-pressed={selected === index} aria-controls={`${id}-${index}`} onClick={() => choose(index)}>{names[index]}{opened.includes(index) && <><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\"> ✓</span><span className=\\"co-sr-only\\">, opened</span></>}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\" role=\\"status\\">Expression {selected + 1} of {expressions.length} · {opened.length} of {expressions.length} opened</p>\\r\\n    {expressions.map((section, index) => <div key={section.props.title} id={`${id}-${index}`} hidden={selected !== index} tabIndex={-1} ref={element => { panels.current[index] = element; }}>{section}</div>)}\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\">{last\\r\\n      ? <p>That was the last of the seven expressions. Choose any of them to read it again, or recall them with the cards below.</p>\\r\\n      : <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-primary\\" onClick={() => choose(selected + 1, true)}>Next expression: {names[selected + 1]}</button>}</div>\\r\\n    {group === \'group-2\' && <OnlyAndOnlyIf/>}\\r\\n    <FamilySupplement group={group}/>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Recall the expressions\\"><p>Once you\'ve read the seven examples, use these cards to recall them. Explain the relationship before turning each card, then compare the complete ideas and the direction with your answer.</p><StudyDeck group={group}/></NoteSection>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// Teach placement and reading through the familiar relationship, then require a decision.\\r\\nfunction MustArrowNote() {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section cr-course-section ma-lesson\\" data-reference-source={8}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>Left: sufficient. Right: necessary.</h2></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-step-body\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ma-lead\\">The arrow points from the <strong>sufficient condition</strong> to the <strong>necessary condition</strong>.</p>\\r\\n      <figure className=\\"ma-example\\" aria-label=\\"If you are enrolled in law school, you must be a student\\">\\r\\n        <figcaption className=\\"ma-read-instruction\\">Read left to right: <strong>“If the left side is true, the right side must be true.”</strong></figcaption>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"ma-relationship\\">\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-condition\\"><span className=\\"ma-side\\">Left: sufficient condition</span><span>Enough to guarantee the right side</span><strong>You are enrolled in law school</strong></div>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><span>must</span><b>⟶</b></div>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-condition\\"><span className=\\"ma-side\\">Right: necessary condition</span><span>Required when the left side is true</span><strong>You are a student</strong></div>\\r\\n        </div>\\r\\n      </figure>\\r\\n      <p>Being enrolled in law school is <strong>enough</strong> to know you are a student. That is why enrollment goes on the <strong>left</strong>. Being a student is <strong>required</strong>, so it goes on the <strong>right</strong>.</p>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-placement\\" aria-label=\\"Place and read the conditions\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Place the conditions. Then read the arrow.</h3>\\r\\n        <p>Choose what belongs on each side, then use the same direction with a new rule.</p>\\r\\n        <p className=\\"ma-compact-note\\">Put the <strong>condition</strong> beside the arrow, not the indicator word. For example, write “you win the raffle,” not “if.”</p>\\r\\n        <MustArrowPractice />\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-order\\" aria-label=\\"Two ways to say the same thing\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Two ways to say the same thing</h3>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>If</strong> you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</p><p>You get a bicycle <strong>if</strong> you win the raffle.</p></div>\\r\\n        <p>Both mean: <strong>You win the raffle → You get a bicycle.</strong> Winning is sufficient, so it goes left, even when it comes last in the sentence.</p>\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ma-takeaway\\"><strong>The arrow guarantees the right side when the left side is true.</strong> Being a student alone does not prove that you are enrolled in law school.</p>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension ma-reverse\\"><summary>Why can\'t we reverse it?</summary><div className=\\"ma-reverse-body\\"><p>A person could be a history student who is not enrolled in law school. That possibility fits our rule.</p><p>The arrow states a relationship. By itself, it does not tell us that either condition is true, or that one causes the other.</p></div></details>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// One translation process, checked four ways, instead of five restarted lessons.\\r\\nfunction WholeMeaningNote() {\\r\\n  const onlyComparison = groupSection(\'group-2\', \'Compare only with the only\');\\r\\n  const insideCheck = Children.toArray(reference(12).props.children);\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check a translation four ways\\">\\r\\n      <p>A diagram can point the right way and still say the wrong thing. Each check below catches a different slip in the same translation process.</p>\\r\\n      <ol>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Target:</strong> did you find the whole idea the indicator refers to, wherever it sits?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Qualification:</strong> did your short label keep every restriction?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Direction:</strong> did a similar-looking expression trick you into the wrong side?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Imagined case:</strong> does your arrow promise something the sentence leaves open?</li>\\r\\n      </ol>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 1: find the whole target, wherever it sits\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The target comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Ask which complete idea the sentence calls required, then give that idea its role.</p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 2: keep every qualification\\">\\r\\n      <p>Once you have the target, shorten it without losing what limits it. These two sentences test the same habit from opposite sides of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\">\\r\\n        <div><blockquote>Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.</blockquote><p>Applicant alone isn\'t the condition. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. A shorter label should save writing, not change the requirement.</p></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. What goes left?\\" options={[\'Applicant\', \'Applicant submits late\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You dropped the late-submission restriction. What goes right?\\" options={[\'Has current pass\', \'Has any pass\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'This keeps the full requirement.
